Comment:  
(NOTE) 
Chronic autoimmune urticaria (CIU) may be associated with  
autoantibodies to the high affinity IgE receptor  
(Fc-epsilon R1) or to IgE. In the presence of these  
autoantibodies, cross-linking of the Fc-epsilon-R1 receptor  
occurs, leading to basophil activation. The laboratory  
tests for the activation of donor basophils by CIU serum by  
analyzing the expression of the basophil specific  
ectoenzyme, CD203c. CD203c is upregulated on the surface of  
basophils following activation. A positive result is  
indicative of the presence of autoantibodies associated  
with CIU, but may also be due to other basophil-activating  
serum factors. Results must be correlated with clinical  
findings. The reference range was developed by the National  
Jewish Health Advanced Diagnostic Laboratories by analyzing  
80 healthy control serum samples. 
REFERENCES:  
Chronic urticaria sera increase basophil CD203c expression.  
Yasnowsky KM1, Dreskin SC, Efaw B, Schoen D, Vedanthan PK,  
Alam R, Harbeck RJ. J Allergy Clin Immunol. 2006  
Jun;117(6):1430-4. 
DISCLAIMER:  
 
This test uses a kit/reagent designated by the manufacturer  
as "for research use, not for clinical use" as well as one  
or more reagents classified as an analyte specific reagent  
(ASR).  The performance characteristics of this test have  
been validated by Advanced Diagnostic Laboratories at  
National Jewish Health.  It has not been cleared or  
approved by the US Food and Drug Administration. The  
results are not intended to be used as the sole means for  
clinical diagnosis or patient management decisions.  This  
laboratory is certified under the Clinical Laboratory  
Improvement Amendments of 1988 (CLIA-88) as qualified to  
perform high complexity clinical laboratory testing. 
 
For additional information, including test methodology,  
please refer to the ADx Test Directory at www.NJLabs.org  
Medical Director: Steve Groshong, M.D., PhD Advanced  
Diagnostic Laboratories, National Jewish Health

Results: 
 
Sudomotor function:  
 
QSART (Q-Sweat®) showed normal sweat volume at all sites except  
the forearm.  This pattern can be seen with some kinds of small  
fiber sympathetic sudomotor cholinergic nerve dysfunction, but  
most commonly is due to interference from medications (e.g.  
antidepressants, anticholinergics) 
 
Cardiovascular autonomic function: 
 
2. Heart rate and Blood Pressure Response to 10 min 70° Head Up  
Tilt (HUT): 
Baseline supine systolic blood (SBP) pressure was ~ 110 mmHg. SBP  
after HUT 130 mmHg 
Baseline supine heart rate was ~ 70 beats per minute (bpm), which  
changed to 85-90 bpm during HUT (normal HR augmentation <40 under  
age 20, <30 in adults) 


=== PAGE 21 ===
Narrative
 
 
3.  Valsalva Maneuver: 
Blood pressure response to Valsalva Maneuver (sympathetic  
noradrenergic vasomotor baroreflex function):  normal phase 2 and  
IV  ; PRT = 1.6 s 
Valsalva ratio:  normal at 2.6. (5th percentile: 1.46) 
 
4.  Heart Rate Variability to Deep Breathing/HRDB (baroreflex  
cardiovagal function):  
 normal with the best heart rate difference of 22.6 bpm. (5th  
percentile: 14 bpm) 
 
 
Conclusions:  
 
These results are essentially normal without evidence of  
sudomotor dysfunction, cardiovagal or vasomotor baroreflex  
dysfunction, orthostatic hypotension, or excessive orthostatic  
tachycardia.  Patchy low sweat volume is of uncertain  
significance, but can be seen due to interference from  
medications (e.g. fluoxetine, naltrexone) or more rarely, small  
fiber sudomotor neuropathy or sweat gland dysfunction. 
 
*It is advised that the report needs to be interpreted by the  
ordering provider or an autonomic expert, taking into account the  
clinical context. 
 
**Please note: published normative data is sparse in pediatric  
age populations.

Composite Autonomic Scoring Scale (Low 1993, PMID: 8392653)   
Sudomotor       
0 normal    
1 single site abnormal on QSART or     
length-dependent pattern (distal < 1/3 of proximal) or  
persistent sweat activity at foot    
2 single site <50% of lower limit on QSART   
3 Two or more sites < 50% lower limit    
Adrenergic Index       
0 normal  
1 Phase IIe decrease <40 but >20 mmHg mean BP or   
Phase II does not return to baseline or    
Decrease in PP to <50% of baseline    
2 Phase IIe decrease <40 but >20 mmHg mean BP and   
phase II late or phase IV absent     
3 phase IIe decrease of >40 mmHg +no late phase II and phase IV  
4 Criteria for 3 + OH (systolic >30, mean decerased >20)  
Cardiovascular HR index       
0 normal  
1 HRDB or VR mildly decreased (above 50% minimum)  
2 HRDB or VR decreased to less than 50% minimum  
3 Both HRDB and VR < 50% minimum    
Total =  2       
>=5 suggests significant autonomic dysfunction MILD = 1-3 MOD =  
4-6  Sev = 7-10  
 
 
 
 
 
Techniques and procedures used in the MGH Autonomic Lab for  
Autonomic Reflex Screen Testing  
 
 
CNAP® (Continuous Noninvasive Arterial Pressure) with double  
finger sensor technology, is used for recording continuous  


=== PAGE 22 ===
Narrative
(beat-to-beat) blood pressure. 3-Lead continuous ECG monitoring  
is used throughout the testing.  
 
Q-Sweat test is performed for evaluation of the integrity of the  
postganglionic sympathetic sudomotor fibers (primarily  
cholinergic), with the use of 10% acetylcholine solution  
iontophoresis technique to measure indirect reflex sweat response  
through changes in humidity, recorded from 4 standard locations  
of ventral forearm, upper lateral shin, lower medial shin and  
dorsal foot. 
 
70° Head-Up-Tilt is performed after 10 minutes of resting quietly  
in supine position with stable baseline BP and HR. Then the  
patient is tilted-up to 70°, and is returned to supine after 10  
min or until patient experiences problematic severe orthostatic  
intolerance, presyncopal symptoms, or syncope, in which case the  
test is terminated prematurely. 
 
Valsalva Maneuver is performed in supine position with expiratory  
pressure to be maintained ideally at 40 mmHg for 15 seconds by  
blowing through a disposable COVID-filtered mouthpiece attached  
to a digital manometer built in WR medical autonomic testing  
equipment. If this pressure cannot be maintained, lesser  
pressures are acceptable only if they are sufficient to elicit  
appropriate physiologic responses. The test is done at least  
twice with 2 min rest period in between, to analyze blood  
pressure changes and pulse valsalva ratio of the best recording.  
In the case of a table top/flat top/square wave response or other  
technical challenges, this maneuver may be repeated again sitting  
up. 
 
Heart rate variability with deep breathing is performed in supine  
position, after a 5 min period of resting, with 5 seconds  
inspiration and 5 seconds expiration cycles for 1 minute (6  
breaths per minute) using timed breathing cues. The test is done  
twice with 2 min rest period in between, to analyze the best  
heart rate changes recorded for a 5 consecutive breathing cycles.

Comment: HbA1c levels 5.7-6.4% represent pre-diabetes, indicating impaired glucose control and an increased risk of 
developing diabetes. The diagnostic HbA1c level for diabetes is 6.5% or greater. HbA1c levels <4.2% may indicate a 
hemoglobinopathy or anemia, and an alternative method is recommended to monitor glucose control.

Comment: The Calculated Mean Blood Glucose (CMBG) represents the estimated average glucose calculated from the measured 
hemoglobin A1c (HbA1c). There is no established normal range for the CMBG, however a 5.6% HbA1c (upper limit of normal) 
represents a CMBG of 114 mg/dL.

-------------------ADDITIONAL INFORMATION------------------- 
PLEASE NOTE:  The given thyroglobulin antibody (TgAb)  
reference cutoff of <4.0 IU/mL is for the evaluation of 
autoimmune thyroiditis.  
 
A cutoff of <1.8 IU/mL may be more suitable for 
the detection of potential thyroglobulin antibody (TgAb)  
interference in thyroglobulin immunoassays. 
 
The thyroglobulin antibody testing method is an  
immunoenzymatic assay manufactured by Beckman Coulter Inc.  
and performed on the Unicel DXI 800. 
 
Values obtained from different assay methods or kits 
may be different and cannot be used interchangeably. 
The results cannot be interpreted as absolute evidence for  
the presence or absence of malignant disease.

IgE receptor antibody (IGE RECEPTOR ANTIBODY) - Final result (02/18/2025 2:53 PM EST)

Value

Pathologist Signature
Basophils (%), CD203c
1.9
0 - 12 %
 
 
MAYO 
REFERRAL
 


=== PAGE 57 ===
 
Component
Value
Ref 
Range
Test Method
Analysis 
Time
Performed 
At
Pathologist Signature
Comment:  
(NOTE) 
Chronic autoimmune urticaria (CIU) may be associated with  
autoantibodies to the high affinity IgE receptor  
(Fc-epsilon R1) or to IgE. In the presence of these  
autoantibodies, cross-linking of the Fc-epsilon-R1 receptor  
occurs, leading to basophil activation. The laboratory  
tests for the activation of donor basophils by CIU serum by  
analyzing the expression of the basophil specific  
ectoenzyme, CD203c. CD203c is upregulated on the surface of  
basophils following activation. A positive result is  
indicative of the presence of autoantibodies associated  
with CIU, but may also be due to other basophil-activating  
serum factors. Results must be correlated with clinical  
findings. The reference range was developed by the National  
Jewish Health Advanced Diagnostic Laboratories by analyzing  
80 healthy control serum samples. 
REFERENCES:  
Chronic urticaria sera increase basophil CD203c expression.  
Yasnowsky KM1, Dreskin SC, Efaw B, Schoen D, Vedanthan PK,  
Alam R, Harbeck RJ. J Allergy Clin Immunol. 2006  
Jun;117(6):1430-4. 
DISCLAIMER:  
 
This test uses a kit/reagent designated by the manufacturer  
as "for research use, not for clinical use" as well as one  
or more reagents classified as an analyte specific reagent  
(ASR).  The performance characteristics of this test have  
been validated by Advanced Diagnostic Laboratories at  
National Jewish Health.  It has not been cleared or  
approved by the US Food and Drug Administration. The  
results are not intended to be used as the sole means for  
clinical diagnosis or patient management decisions.  This  
laboratory is certified under the Clinical Laboratory  
Improvement Amendments of 1988 (CLIA-88) as qualified to  
perform high complexity clinical laboratory testing. 
 
For additional information, including test methodology,  
please refer to the ADx Test Directory at www.NJLabs.org  
Medical Director: Steve Groshong, M.D., PhD Advanced  
Diagnostic Laboratories, National Jewish Health

No

commentary 
necessary.

Reason for study: Hannah Munguia-Flores is a 29 y.o. female  
referred by Lauren Major PA for autonomic evaluation for fatigue,  
exercise intolerance, chronic hives, post-COVID fatigue, and  
autonomic neuropathy. 
 
Location of testing: MGH

Results: 
 
Sudomotor function:  
 
QSART (Q-Sweat®) showed normal sweat volume at all sites except  
the forearm.  This pattern can be seen with some kinds of small  
fiber sympathetic sudomotor cholinergic nerve dysfunction, but  
most commonly is due to interference from medications (e.g.  
antidepressants, anticholinergics) 
 
Cardiovascular autonomic function: 
 
2. Heart rate and Blood Pressure Response to 10 min 70° Head Up  
Tilt (HUT): 
Baseline supine systolic blood (SBP) pressure was ~ 110 mmHg. SBP  
after HUT 130 mmHg 
Baseline supine heart rate was ~ 70 beats per minute (bpm), which  
changed to 85-90 bpm during HUT (normal HR augmentation <40 under  
age 20, <30 in adults) 


=== PAGE 62 ===
Narrative
 
 
3.  Valsalva Maneuver: 
Blood pressure response to Valsalva Maneuver (sympathetic  
noradrenergic vasomotor baroreflex function):  normal phase 2 and  
IV  ; PRT = 1.6 s 
Valsalva ratio:  normal at 2.6. (5th percentile: 1.46) 
 
4.  Heart Rate Variability to Deep Breathing/HRDB (baroreflex  
cardiovagal function):  
 normal with the best heart rate difference of 22.6 bpm. (5th  
percentile: 14 bpm) 
 
 
Conclusions:  
 
These results are essentially normal without evidence of  
sudomotor dysfunction, cardiovagal or vasomotor baroreflex  
dysfunction, orthostatic hypotension, or excessive orthostatic  
tachycardia.  Patchy low sweat volume is of uncertain  
significance, but can be seen due to interference from  
medications (e.g. fluoxetine, naltrexone) or more rarely, small  
fiber sudomotor neuropathy or sweat gland dysfunction. 
 
*It is advised that the report needs to be interpreted by the  
ordering provider or an autonomic expert, taking into account the  
clinical context. 
 
**Please note: published normative data is sparse in pediatric  
age populations.

Composite Autonomic Scoring Scale (Low 1993, PMID: 8392653)   
Sudomotor       
0 normal    
1 single site abnormal on QSART or     
length-dependent pattern (distal < 1/3 of proximal) or  
persistent sweat activity at foot    
2 single site <50% of lower limit on QSART   
3 Two or more sites < 50% lower limit    
Adrenergic Index       
0 normal  
1 Phase IIe decrease <40 but >20 mmHg mean BP or   
Phase II does not return to baseline or    
Decrease in PP to <50% of baseline    
2 Phase IIe decrease <40 but >20 mmHg mean BP and   
phase II late or phase IV absent     
3 phase IIe decrease of >40 mmHg +no late phase II and phase IV  
4 Criteria for 3 + OH (systolic >30, mean decerased >20)  
Cardiovascular HR index       
0 normal  
1 HRDB or VR mildly decreased (above 50% minimum)  
2 HRDB or VR decreased to less than 50% minimum  
3 Both HRDB and VR < 50% minimum    
Total =  2       
>=5 suggests significant autonomic dysfunction MILD = 1-3 MOD =  
4-6  Sev = 7-10  
 
 
 
 
 
Techniques and procedures used in the MGH Autonomic Lab for  
Autonomic Reflex Screen Testing  
 
 
CNAP® (Continuous Noninvasive Arterial Pressure) with double  
finger sensor technology, is used for recording continuous  


=== PAGE 63 ===
Narrative
(beat-to-beat) blood pressure. 3-Lead continuous ECG monitoring  
is used throughout the testing.  
 
Q-Sweat test is performed for evaluation of the integrity of the  
postganglionic sympathetic sudomotor fibers (primarily  
cholinergic), with the use of 10% acetylcholine solution  
iontophoresis technique to measure indirect reflex sweat response  
through changes in humidity, recorded from 4 standard locations  
of ventral forearm, upper lateral shin, lower medial shin and  
dorsal foot. 
 
70° Head-Up-Tilt is performed after 10 minutes of resting quietly  
in supine position with stable baseline BP and HR. Then the  
patient is tilted-up to 70°, and is returned to supine after 10  
min or until patient experiences problematic severe orthostatic  
intolerance, presyncopal symptoms, or syncope, in which case the  
test is terminated prematurely. 
 
Valsalva Maneuver is performed in supine position with expiratory  
pressure to be maintained ideally at 40 mmHg for 15 seconds by  
blowing through a disposable COVID-filtered mouthpiece attached  
to a digital manometer built in WR medical autonomic testing  
equipment. If this pressure cannot be maintained, lesser  
pressures are acceptable only if they are sufficient to elicit  
appropriate physiologic responses. The test is done at least  
twice with 2 min rest period in between, to analyze blood  
pressure changes and pulse valsalva ratio of the best recording.  
In the case of a table top/flat top/square wave response or other  
technical challenges, this maneuver may be repeated again sitting  
up. 
 
Heart rate variability with deep breathing is performed in supine  
position, after a 5 min period of resting, with 5 seconds  
inspiration and 5 seconds expiration cycles for 1 minute (6  
breaths per minute) using timed breathing cues. The test is done  
twice with 2 min rest period in between, to analyze the best  
heart rate changes recorded for a 5 consecutive breathing cycles.

Results: 
 
Sudomotor function:  
 
QSART (Q-Sweat®) showed normal sweat volume at all sites except  
the forearm. This pattern can be seen with some kinds of small  
fiber sympathetic sudomotor cholinergic nerve dysfunction, but  
most commonly is due to interference from medications (e.g.  
antidepressants, anticholinergics) 
 
Cardiovascular autonomic function: 
 
2. Heart rate and Blood Pressure Response to 10 min 70° Head Up  
Tilt (HUT): 
Baseline supine systolic blood (SBP) pressure was ~ 110 mmHg. SBP  
after HUT 130 mmHg 
Baseline supine heart rate was ~ 70 beats per minute (bpm), which  
changed to 85-90 bpm during HUT (normal HR augmentation <40 under  
age 20, <30 in adults) 
 
3. Valsalva Maneuver: 
Blood pressure response to Valsalva Maneuver (sympathetic  
noradrenergic vasomotor baroreflex function): normal phase 2 and  
IV ; PRT = 1.6 s 
Valsalva ratio: normal at 2.6. (5th percentile: 1.46) 
 
4. Heart Rate Variability to Deep Breathing/HRDB (baroreflex  
cardiovagal function):  
normal with the best heart rate difference of 22.6 bpm. (5th  
percentile: 14 bpm) 
 
Conclusions:  
 
These results are essentially normal without evidence of  
sudomotor dysfunction, cardiovagal or vasomotor baroreflex  
dysfunction, orthostatic hypotension, or excessive orthostatic  
tachycardia. Patchy low sweat volume is of uncertain  
significance, but can be seen due to interference from  
medications (e.g. fluoxetine, naltrexone) or more rarely, small  
fiber sudomotor neuropathy or sweat gland dysfunction.

Techniques and procedures used in the MGH Autonomic Lab for  
Autonomic Reflex Screen Testing  
 
CNAP® (Continuous Noninvasive Arterial Pressure) with double  
finger sensor technology, is used for recording continuous  
(beat-to-beat) blood pressure. 3-Lead continuous ECG monitoring  
is used throughout the testing.  
 
Q-Sweat test is performed for evaluation of the integrity of the  
postganglionic sympathetic sudomotor fibers (primarily  
cholinergic), with the use of 10% acetylcholine solution  
iontophoresis technique to measure indirect reflex sweat response  
through changes in humidity, recorded from 4 standard locations  
of ventral forearm, upper lateral shin, lower medial shin and  
dorsal foot. 
 
70° Head-Up-Tilt is performed after 10 minutes of resting quietly  
in supine position with stable baseline BP and HR. Then the  
patient is tilted-up to 70°, and is returned to supine after 10  
min or until patient experiences problematic severe orthostatic  
intolerance, presyncopal symptoms, or syncope, in which case the  
test is terminated prematurely. 
 
Valsalva Maneuver is performed in supine position with expiratory  
pressure to be maintained ideally at 40 mmHg for 15 seconds by  
blowing through a disposable COVID-filtered mouthpiece attached  
to a digital manometer built in WR medical autonomic testing  
equipment. If this pressure cannot be maintained, lesser  
pressures are acceptable only if they are sufficient to elicit  
appropriate physiologic responses. The test is done at least  
twice with 2 min rest period in between, to analyze blood  
pressure changes and pulse valsalva ratio of the best recording.  
In the case of a table top/flat top/square wave response or other  
technical challenges, this maneuver may be repeated again sitting  
up. 
 
Heart rate variability with deep breathing is performed in supine  
position, after a 5 min period of resting, with 5 seconds  
inspiration and 5 seconds expiration cycles for 1 minute (6  
breaths per minute) using timed breathing cues. The test is done  
twice with 2 min rest period in between, to analyze the best  
heart rate changes recorded for a 5 consecutive breathing cycles. 
 
 
 
ASSESSMENT:  
29 y.o. year old female with PMH significant for asthma, dysautonomia, POTS, MDD, who presents with Long Covid syndrome as 
described below. Referred by Pulm for evaluation and management, specifically for the consideration of stellate ganglion block.

were answered.

documentation.  
 
History of Present Illness  
 
? 
History of Present Illness 
The patient is a 30-year-old female presenting for her annual physical exam with a complex history including persistent fatigue, 
post-mononucleosis symptoms, orthostatic intolerance, postural orthostatic tachycardia syndrome (POTS), urticaria, asthma, and 
iron deficiency. 
 
Fatigue and Post-Mononucleosis Symptoms 
- Fatigue has persisted for 2 years following severe mononucleosis contracted during graduate school, about 1 month after her first 
COVID-19 infection. 
- Persistent symptoms led to medical leave from MIT and participation in the long COVID clinic at Mass General. 
- She is also under the care of a neuropsychiatrist specializing in long COVID and ME/CFS. 
- Fatigue is significant, often requiring her to remain horizontal. 
- She reports little improvement over 2 years despite adherence to POTS protocols and treatments, including stellate ganglion 
blocks. 
 
Orthostatic Intolerance and Postural Orthostatic Tachycardia Syndrome (POTS) 
- Orthostatic intolerance and POTS were evaluated with a tilt table test; criteria were not met. 
- She completed POTS physical therapy at MGH, which was helpful. 
- Propranolol was started by neuropsychiatrist for increased heart rate with a target of 60 mg. 
- She reports feeling unwell since the dose increase last week. 
 
Urticaria 
- Urticaria was diagnosed at the long COVID clinic. 
- Urticaria persists despite monthly Xolair injections, which she finds ineffective. 
 
Asthma 
- Asthma was diagnosed at the long COVID clinic. 
- Asthma is well controlled, with no symptoms for over 5 years. 
 
Iron Deficiency 
- Iron deficiency is ongoing; she received an iron infusion around 12/2025. 
- She requested iron testing. 
 
Ear Discomfort 
- She reports ear discomfort and pain, with both ears feeling clogged. 
- The long COVID clinic advised primary care evaluation. 
 
Gynecological History 
- She does not perform self-breast exams and has no history of abnormal Pap smears. 
- Last Pap smear was approximately 1 year ago. 
- She requests a birth control prescription, previously managed by her prior primary care clinician. 
 
Other Relevant History 


=== PAGE 139 ===
- No migraines or auras. 
- She reports significant stress regarding the need to return to MIT in the fall or withdraw due to medical leave duration limits. 
- Only one semester remains to complete her degree.

PROCEDURE: 
Stellate Ganglion Block (Repeat) 
 
Date/Time: 6/12/2026 1:57 PM 
 
Performed by: Kim, Alexander James, MD 
Authorized by: Kim, Alexander James, MD  
Universal Protocol:  
Verbal consent obtained: Yes  
Written consent obtained: Yes  
Time out: Immediately prior to the procedure, a time out was called to verify that there is a signed consent form and that the 
correct patient, planned procedure, site and side are consistent with documentation and that necessary equipment and/or blood 
products are available prior to the start of the case.  
 
Preparation:  
Preparation: Patient was prepped and draped in usual sterile fashion  
Estimated blood loss: less than 10cc  
 
Anesthesia:  
Local anesthesia used?: Yes  
 
Sedation:  
Patient sedated?: No  
 
Post-procedure:  
Patient tolerance: patient tolerated the procedure well with no immediate complications 
 
Diagnosis: Autonomic dysfunction 
 
Stellate Ganglion Block, RIGHT 
After obtaining consent, pre-procedure pulse and blood pressure were stable and available in the patient's chart for review. Skin 
temperature probes were attached to both upper extremities. The patient was placed in the supine position with a thoracolumbar 
roll. The patient was prepped and draped in a sterile manner. The neck anatomy was identified with the ultrasound machine. The 
carotid artery, trachea and longus colli muscle were identified. A 22G spinal needle was inserted and advanced in real time under 


=== PAGE 160 ===
ultrasound guidance to the body of the longus colli muscle. There was no evidence of paresthesia, heme, or CSF. An injection was 
performed with 9 ml of 0.25% Bupivicaine and 10 mg of dexamethasone. This was administered over a two to three minute period 
with frequent aspirations, all of which were negative. Post procedure patient had a positive Horner's syndrome. Patient had an 
increase in temperature of arm, and reported improvement of pain. There was no evidence of motor blockade. The patient 
tolerated the procedure well. Following the procedure the patient's vital signs were stable. The intravenous was removed. The 
patient was discharged home in good condition after being given discharge instructions. 
 
Time Out: Immediately prior to the procedure, the following was verbally confirmed that there is a consent form and that the 
correct patient, planned procedure, site and side are consistent with documentation and that necessary equipment and/or blood 
products are available prior to the start of the case. 
 
Complications: none 
EBL: <5 cc

Michael Glicksman.

PROCEDURE: 
Stellate ganglion - 30 
 
Date/Time: 5/29/2026 12:53 PM

Universal Protocol:  
Verbal consent obtained: Yes

correct patient, planned procedure, site and side are consistent with documentation and that necessary equipment and/or blood 
products are available prior to the start of the case.  
 
Preparation:  
Preparation: Patient was prepped and draped in usual sterile fashion  
Estimated blood loss: less than 10cc  
 
Anesthesia:  
Local anesthesia used?: Yes  
 
Sedation:  
Patient sedated?: No  
 
Post-procedure:  
Patient tolerance: patient tolerated the procedure well with no immediate complications 
 
Diagnosis: Autonomic dysfunction 
 
Stellate Ganglion Block, LEFT 


=== PAGE 173 ===
After obtaining consent, pre-procedure pulse and blood pressure were stable and available in the patient's chart for review. Skin 
temperature probes were attached to both upper extremities. The patient was placed in the supine position with a thoracolumbar 
roll. The patient was prepped and draped in a sterile manner. The neck anatomy was identified with the ultrasound machine. The 
carotid artery, trachea and longus colli muscle were identified. A 22G spinal needle was inserted and advanced in real time under 
ultrasound guidance to the body of the longus colli muscle. There was no evidence of paresthesia, heme, or CSF. An injection was 
performed with 9 ml of 0.25% Bupivicaine and 10 mg of dexamethasone. This was administered over a two to three minute period 
with frequent aspirations, all of which were negative. Post procedure patient had a positive Horner's syndrome. Patient had an 
increase in temperature of arm, and reported improvement of pain. There was no evidence of motor blockade. The patient 
tolerated the procedure well. Following the procedure the patient's vital signs were stable. The intravenous was removed. The 
patient was discharged home in good condition after being given discharge instructions. 
 
Time Out: Immediately prior to the procedure, the following was verbally confirmed that there is a consent form and that the 
correct patient, planned procedure, site and side are consistent with documentation and that necessary equipment and/or blood 
products are available prior to the start of the case. 
 
Complications: none 
EBL: <5 cc

Cardiac Studies: ?Personally reviewed; see EMR for complete details  
 
AFT: 2/3/2025: 
Conclusions:  
These results are essentially normal without evidence of sudomotor dysfunction, cardiovagal or vasomotor baroreflex dysfunction, 
orthostatic hypotension, or excessive orthostatic tachycardia. Patchy low sweat volume is of uncertain significance, but can be seen 
due to interference from medications (e.g. fluoxetine, naltrexone) or more rarely, small fiber sudomotor neuropathy or sweat gland 
dysfunction. 
 
Assessment & Plan 
 
Autonomic dysfunction: 
Given the constellation of symptoms including hives, neuropathy, brain fog at times, and post exertional fatigue, all symptoms 
suggestive of autonomic dysfunction.  
Recent Aft was overall reassuring, with possible small fiber sudomotor neuropathy or sweat gland dysfunction  
We reviewed the pathophysiology in detail with the patient today.  
We provided an educational document that goes over this, in addition to some tips and behavioral changes including proper 
hydration, salt intake to at least 2000-8000 mg per day or 4 teaspoons, compression stocking and modified exercise regimen with 
emphasis on recumbent exercises and pacing strategies.  
Graded exercise program = gentle exercise every day, not so much to make you crash, but a little bit consistently every day then 
extend it once or twice a week by increasing *either* the duration or the intensity a little bit 
- gentle walks  
- recumbent exercise (jumping jacks on your back, recumbent bicycle, etc)  
- swimming or walking in the shallow end of the pool 
- AVOID exercises where you are holding your breath (push-ups, sit-ups, etc) 
- Pacing yourself: Spoon Theory 
- Recommend taking cognitive breaks, for about 1 minute every 30-60 minutes 
- importance of hydration and salt intake 
- compression stockings (15-20 mmHg)  
- resources: packet on Chronic Fatigue Syndrome / Myalgic Encephalopathy; website www.dysautonomiainternational.org  
Recommend considering participation in Long Covid clinical trials, including RECOVER trials available in CRC resources 
Will re-refer Long-Covid PT and cognitive rehab 
 
Was seen by Dr. Gupta in NP based on hx of ADHD, restarted adderall 15mg although struggling with side effects. Recommend she 
pursue seeking LT psychiatrist.  
 
In the future, we can consider referral to pain med for stellate ganglion block, discussed following up with them 
 
Continues to follow with NT as well, on 4.5mg LDN for 1 month now. 
 
Recent labs reassuring 
Continues to follow with allergy for hives/allergies/?mcas. Recent IgE 289 
 
OSA:  
- Given her symptoms of snoring, witnessed apnea, non restorative sleep, excessive daytime sleepiness and fatigue, OSA is 
suspected 
- Recently completed sleep study. Ahi 5/hr, most events while supine 
- We reviewed the pathophysiology of untreated OSA including its link to cardiovascular disease, neurocognitive dysfunction, 
metabolic disorders, as well as excessive daytime sleepiness and fatigue 
- Wt loss was encouraged 
- Avoid sleep in the supine position as much as tolerated 
- Avoid alcohol and sedatives and avoid driving if feeling drowsy 
? 
The patient was also counseled about general good sleep hygiene practices, including allowing for adequate total sleep time and 
no daytime napping.

and hives daily.

Prior History from 2/18/25 visit:

She has had hives all her life

frequency, and more so since last Spring 2024.

and was treated with a course of antibiotics. 
The hives are slightly raised, erythematous, pruritic. Each lesion lasts only a few hours.

IMPRESSION AND PLAN: 
Hannah Munguia-Flores is a 30 y.o. female presenting with: 
 
Chronic spontaneous urticaria: 
Her hives and pruritus are not well controlled on high dose oral antihistamines. Prior workup showed normal thyroid function, and 
negative CU index and IgE receptor antibody testing. 
Given uncontrolled symptoms with high dose antihistamines, I recommend stepping up therapy to Xolair/Omalizumab. We 
reviewed Omalizumab is a monoclonal antibody against IgE, approved for the treatment of CSU that is not controlled with oral 
antihistamine therapy. Omalizumab has not been shown to have a long-term disease-modifying effect, so patients may relapse 
when omalizumab is tapered or discontinued. However, CSU is a disorder that remits or resolves in the majority of patients within a 
few years even if untreated. From all nonantihistamine therapies that have proven efficacy for refractory CSU, omalizumab is the 
best studied, most widely used therapy and has a very low incidence of serious adverse effects. After reviewing benefits vs risks, 
Hannah agrees to start therapy with Xolair. 
PLAN: 
- Start Xolair 300 mg every 28 days. Consent signed today. First 3 doses will be administered in clinic 
- Epi pen will be prescribed given black box warning for anaphylaxis 
- Continue Allegra 360 mg BID 
- Continue Pepcid 20 mg BID 
- Based on her response to Xolair, we will consider reducing her oral antihistamines, starting with Pepcid 
? 
2. Chronic rhinitis, otalgia 
Rhinitis symptoms are mild--she has mild inferior turbinate hypertrophy on exam. She has bilateral persistent ear pain 
- Consider intranasal corticosteroid: [Flonase / Nasacort / Rhinocort] 2 sprays per nostril daily. Importance of daily use for at least 2 
weeks for clinical benefit was reviewed. We also discussed adequate technique: First blow your nose gently, and apply by inserting 
the tip of the nasal spray pointing "up and out" towards the outer corner of your eye. After spraying, do a gentle sniff so as to 
distribute it evenly and prevent it from accumulating in the back of the throat. 
- Recommend follow up with PCP for further evaluation of ear pain  
 
We have asked for follow up in 6 months. The patient was counseled and all questions were answered. Thank you for allowing us to 
share in Hannah Munguia-Flores's care.  
 
I personally spent 40 minutes preparing for, caring for the patient (both face-to-face and non-face-to-face) and finalizing the visit 
for this patient. 
 
Lourdes Ramirez, MD

AFT: 2/3/2025: 
Conclusions:  


=== PAGE 280 ===
These results are essentially normal without evidence of sudomotor dysfunction, cardiovagal or vasomotor baroreflex dysfunction, 
orthostatic hypotension, or excessive orthostatic tachycardia. Patchy low sweat volume is of uncertain significance, but can be seen 
due to interference from medications (e.g. fluoxetine, naltrexone) or more rarely, small fiber sudomotor neuropathy or sweat gland 
dysfunction. 
 
Assessment & Plan 
 
Autonomic dysfunction: 
Given the constellation of symptoms including hives, neuropathy, brain fog at times, and post exertional fatigue, all symptoms 
suggestive of autonomic dysfunction.  
Recent Aft was overall reassuring, with possible small fiber sudomotor neuropathy or sweat gland dysfunction  
We reviewed the pathophysiology in detail with the patient today.  
We provided an educational document that goes over this, in addition to some tips and behavioral changes including proper 
hydration, salt intake to at least 2000-8000 mg per day or 4 teaspoons, compression stocking and modified exercise regimen with 
emphasis on recumbent exercises and pacing strategies.  
Graded exercise program = gentle exercise every day, not so much to make you crash, but a little bit consistently every day then 
extend it once or twice a week by increasing *either* the duration or the intensity a little bit 
- gentle walks  
- recumbent exercise (jumping jacks on your back, recumbent bicycle, etc)  
- swimming or walking in the shallow end of the pool 
- AVOID exercises where you are holding your breath (push-ups, sit-ups, etc) 
- Pacing yourself: Spoon Theory 
- Recommend taking cognitive breaks, for about 1 minute every 30-60 minutes 
- importance of hydration and salt intake 
- compression stockings (15-20 mmHg)  
- resources: packet on Chronic Fatigue Syndrome / Myalgic Encephalopathy; website www.dysautonomiainternational.org  
Recommend considering participation in Long Covid clinical trials, including RECOVER trials available in CRC resources 
Pursuing Long-Covid PT and cognitive rehab 
 
Recently seen by Dr. Gupta in NP based on hx of ADHD, restarted adderall 15mg although struggling with side effects. Recommend

AFT: 2/3/2025: 
Conclusions:  
These results are essentially normal without evidence of sudomotor dysfunction, cardiovagal or vasomotor baroreflex dysfunction, 
orthostatic hypotension, or excessive orthostatic tachycardia. Patchy low sweat volume is of uncertain significance, but can be seen 
due to interference from medications (e.g. fluoxetine, naltrexone) or more rarely, small fiber sudomotor neuropathy or sweat gland 
dysfunction. 
 
ASSESSMENT: 
Hannah Munguia-Flores is a 29 y.o. female with a PMH of POTS, presenting brain fog and worsening POTS symptoms following 
COVID infection, consistent with Long COVID syndrome.  
 
The patient has experienced limitations in quality of life and function due to the symptoms of brain fog and fatigue as a sequelae 
of COVID-19 infection. The stellate ganglion block has been found to improve the sense of smell and taste as well as a number of 
other Long COVID symptoms in some patients. This has been studied and documented in a case series (Liu, et al. Stellate ganglion 
block reduces symptoms of Long COVID: A case series. J Neuroimmunology, 2022).  
 
As the patient's symptoms have been persistent and refractory to conservative measures including prescribed supplementation, 
sensory retraining/PT, low-dose naltrexone, we recommend proceeding with a trial of a stellate ganglion block.  
 
The details of the Stellate Ganglion Block as well as the risks/benefits/alternatives were discussed. The patient asked appropriate 
and insightful questions and demonstrated understanding and synthesis of the presented information. The patient elected to 
proceed.  
 
Diagnosis: 
Autonomic dysfunction 
Long COVID 
Myofascial pain  
Tension headaches 
POTS 


=== PAGE 301 ===
 
ASSESSMENT/PLAN:  
29 y.o. year old female with PMH significant for asthma, dysautonomia, POTS, MDD, who presents with Long Covid syndrome as 
described below. Referred by Pulm for evaluation and management, specifically for the consideration of stellate ganglion block. 
 
Clinical presentation and diagnostics are consistent with likely Long Covid syndrome.  
 
Discussed at length regarding risk and benefits of conservative management and more aggressive interventional approach 
(including stellate ganglion block) as part of multimodal therapy. Patient verbalized understanding and agreed. All her questions 
were answered.  
 
Diagnosis: Long Covid syndrome, autonomic dysfunction 
Continue medications as prescribed by her providers.  
Continue HEP 
RTC for R sided STG (does not endorse laterality to her symptoms, can proceed with side of her choice) 
Plan to proceed with injection on the third floor, with Dr. Sutton or Dr. Ang. She overall is feeling marginal/gradual improvement in 
her symptoms in recent weeks/months so we will hold off on scheduling until she has a better sense of whether these symptoms 
are starting to resolve spontaneously. We discussed that stellate ganglion blocks may not alleviate her symptoms. She is on a leave 
of absence from graduate school (aerospace engineering), but leave is limited and she must make a decision about returning in 
September 2025 or forfeiting her graduate degree progress thus far. She is going to San Diego in June, will return in July and seek 
care with third floor physicians as needed for stellate ganglion block. 
Discussed in detail risks/benefits. Not on blood thinners. Knows to have a driver day of procedure, knows to reschedule if on any 
antibiotics. 
 
I counseled the patient on the importance of health promotion and lifestyle modifications. We also discussed risks/benefits/ 
alternatives to medication management, interventions, physical or occupational therapy, psychological pain management and 
surgical options as appropriate.  
 
The above plan and management options were discussed with patient. The patient is in agreement with the above and verbalized 
understanding.

Continues to struggle the most with intense fatigue.

depressed in the evenings. It increases her HR, also has reduced her appetite. Was followed by psych at MIT. Also feels it does make 
her feel more tired 1-2 days later. Has only taken it once this week. 
She has been referred for physical therapy, and cognitive therapy. Has not been able to start yet.  
Continues on LDN, feels it has helped temp dysregulation. When on Adderall, feels more like she has hot flashes. Has helped with 
overall muscle pain, not so much energy. Started this in January  
Has had fevers of of 99.5, although had a cold, possible GI virus. 
Feels like it is still hard to think clearly, somewhat better with the Adderall.  
She has been told that she snores at night, especially since having Covid. Continues to be fatigued, has difficulty sleeping.

AFT: 2/3/2025: 
Conclusions:  
These results are essentially normal without evidence of sudomotor dysfunction, cardiovagal or vasomotor baroreflex dysfunction, 
orthostatic hypotension, or excessive orthostatic tachycardia. Patchy low sweat volume is of uncertain significance, but can be seen 
due to interference from medications (e.g. fluoxetine, naltrexone) or more rarely, small fiber sudomotor neuropathy or sweat gland 
dysfunction. 
 
Assessment & Plan 
 
Autonomic dysfunction: 
Given the constellation of symptoms including hives, neuropathy, brain fog at times, and post exertional fatigue, all symptoms 
suggestive of autonomic dysfunction.  
Recent Aft was overall reassuring, with possible small fiber sudomotor neuropathy or sweat gland dysfunction  
We reviewed the pathophysiology in detail with the patient today.  
We provided an educational document that goes over this, in addition to some tips and behavioral changes including proper 
hydration, salt intake to at least 2000-8000 mg per day or 4 teaspoons, compression stocking and modified exercise regimen with 
emphasis on recumbent exercises and pacing strategies.  
Graded exercise program = gentle exercise every day, not so much to make you crash, but a little bit consistently every day then 
extend it once or twice a week by increasing *either* the duration or the intensity a little bit 
- gentle walks  
- recumbent exercise (jumping jacks on your back, recumbent bicycle, etc)  
- swimming or walking in the shallow end of the pool 
- AVOID exercises where you are holding your breath (push-ups, sit-ups, etc) 
- Pacing yourself: Spoon Theory 
- Recommend taking cognitive breaks, for about 1 minute every 30-60 minutes 
- importance of hydration and salt intake 
- compression stockings (15-20 mmHg)  
- resources: packet on Chronic Fatigue Syndrome / Myalgic Encephalopathy; website www.dysautonomiainternational.org  
Recommend considering participation in Long Covid clinical trials, including RECOVER trials available in CRC resources 
Pursuing Long-Covid PT and cognitive rehab 
We will order AFT 
 
Recent seen by Dr. Gupta in NP based on hx of ADHD, restarted adderall 15mg although struggling with side effects. Recommend 
she pursue seeking LT physchiatrist. Will discuss with Dr. Gupta regarding Adderall side effects and potential of reducing dose/ 
other medication options. 
 
In the future, we can consider referral to pain med for stellate ganglion block, 
Or to Osher Center as well 
 
Continues to follow with NT as well, on 4.5mg LDN for 1 month now. 
 
We discussed diagnostic work up including level 3 CPET.. We could pursue if continues to have symptoms despite above 
interventions. 
 
Recent labs reassuring 
 
Continues to follow with allergy for hives/allergies/?mcas. Recent IgE 289 
 
OSA:  
- Given his symptoms of snoring, witnessed apnea, non restorative sleep, excessive daytime sleepiness and fatigue, OSA is 
suspected

proceed with an in-lab study  
- We reviewed the pathophysiology of untreated OSA including its link to cardiovascular disease, neurocognitive dysfunction, 
metabolic disorders, as well as excessive daytime sleepiness and fatigue 
- Wt loss was encouraged 
- Avoid sleep in the supine position as much as tolerated 
- Avoid alcohol and sedatives and avoid driving if feeling drowsy

Prior History from 2/18/25 visit: 
 
Chronic hives: 
She has had hives all her life 
When she was a child they almost exclusively occurred at night and she managed them with colloidal oatmeal hives with relief. 
Over the years the hives were mild, but since getting COVID the summer of 2022, the hives have worsened in intensity and 
frequency, and more so since last Spring 2024. 
After getting COVID, she tested + for mono. She saw a naturopathic provider and reportedly tested + for Lyme, babesia, bartonella 
and was treated with a course of antibiotics. 
The hives are slightly raised, erythematous, pruritic. Each lesion lasts only a few hours. 
 
She ordered a serum IgE respiratory panel in May 2024 via Quest diagnostics and only tested positive to dust mites (0.18 sIgE). She 
also ordered food IgE testing which was negative. She also ordered Celiac testing which was negative. IgA level was above normal 
range at 414. She had H.pylori testing which was negative in May 2024. 
 
Location: face, trunk, extremities - hives cover a lot of her skin surface.

Impression/Recommendations  
Formulation:  
Hannah Munguia-Flores is a 29 y.o. female, currently on leave from MIT where she is a graduate student, with a history of ADHD, 
depression, exercise induced asthma, chronic spontaneous urticaria, no prior IP psychiatric hospitalizations or SA, who presents for 
initial consultation in the COVID Recovery Clinic for fatigue.  
 
Persistent physical and cognitive fatigue with exertional component following COVID infection in 2022 with periodic exacerbations 
and resulting functional impairment has been suggestive of a post COVID syndrome. She has a history of ADHD since childhood 
and chronic inattention well controlled with stimulants, which have also been helpful for fatigue in past, though has not been 
taking it outside of her graduate work or since being on leave. Given her reported improvement with psychostimulants for fatigue 
and long term prescription, would be reasonable for her to take prescribed Adderall ER daily (at lower dose of 10mg per her 
preference) to address current physical and cognitive fatigue symptoms, with uptitration as tolerated. Discussed that the evidence 
for effectiveness of supplements in post COVID syndrome is limited and mixed, though given their minimal risks, combination 
CoQ10 and alpha lipoic acid may also be worth trialing for fatigue. There have been no additional or change in cognitive 
symptoms. Prior history is consistent with MDD, though her mood and accompanying anxiety have remained stable on prozac 
since 2021 without current evidence of depression. While possible, fatigue/drowsiness as an effect of prozac is less common, prior 
to COVID infection she tolerated this medication well, and she would prefer to continue it at this time. Discussed impression and 
the below recommendations during consultation. 
 
Working Diagnoses: 
Post COVID syndrome 
Major depressive disorder, in sustained remission 


=== PAGE 385 ===
 
Recommendations: 
 
Diagnostics/Workup 
While she does not have other OSA risk factors, can consider polysomnography given new onset snoring with persistent fatigue/ 
reports of sleepiness during day 
 
Therapeutics 
Pharmacology 
Consider resuming daily use of Adderall ER at 10mg daily to address fatigue given she is already prescribed this medication (at 
higher dose) for ADHD but has not recently been taking. Uptitration as tolerated. 
Consider trialing combination coenzyme Q10 and alpha lipoic acid for fatigue, starting at CoQ 100mg qAM and ALA 100mg BID 
May continue with low dose naltrexone 4.5mg qAM for persistent fatigue given some reported improvement with this. 
May continue with fluoxetine at current dose given stability of mood on this medication. 
 
Referrals 
Can consider referral to physical therapy if concern for deconditioning  
Consider participation in cognitive rehabilitation (previously referred to Spaulding) 
 
Logistics 
Neuropsychiatric evaluation in the COVID Recovery Clinic occurs on a consultative basis. Findings and recommends will be shared 
with the referring provider, the COVID Recovery Clinic, and the patient's PCP. 
Additional consultation visits in the future as needed

IgE receptor antibody (IGE RECEPTOR ANTIBODY) - Final result (02/18/2025 2:53 PM EST)
Component
Value
Ref 
Range
Test Method
Analysis 
Time
Performed 
At
Pathologist Signature
Basophils (%), CD203c
1.9
0 - 12 %
 
 
MAYO 
REFERRAL
 
Comment:  
(NOTE) 
Chronic autoimmune urticaria (CIU) may be associated with  
autoantibodies to the high affinity IgE receptor  
(Fc-epsilon R1) or to IgE. In the presence of these  
autoantibodies, cross-linking of the Fc-epsilon-R1 receptor  
occurs, leading to basophil activation. The laboratory  
tests for the activation of donor basophils by CIU serum by  
analyzing the expression of the basophil specific  
ectoenzyme, CD203c. CD203c is upregulated on the surface of  
basophils following activation. A positive result is  
indicative of the presence of autoantibodies associated  
with CIU, but may also be due to other basophil-activating  
serum factors. Results must be correlated with clinical  
findings. The reference range was developed by the National  
Jewish Health Advanced Diagnostic Laboratories by analyzing  
80 healthy control serum samples. 
REFERENCES:  
Chronic urticaria sera increase basophil CD203c expression.  
Yasnowsky KM1, Dreskin SC, Efaw B, Schoen D, Vedanthan PK,  
Alam R, Harbeck RJ. J Allergy Clin Immunol. 2006  
Jun;117(6):1430-4. 
DISCLAIMER:  
 
This test uses a kit/reagent designated by the manufacturer  
as "for research use, not for clinical use" as well as one  
or more reagents classified as an analyte specific reagent  
(ASR).  The performance characteristics of this test have  
been validated by Advanced Diagnostic Laboratories at  
National Jewish Health.  It has not been cleared or  
approved by the US Food and Drug Administration. The  
results are not intended to be used as the sole means for  
clinical diagnosis or patient management decisions.  This  
laboratory is certified under the Clinical Laboratory  
Improvement Amendments of 1988 (CLIA-88) as qualified to  
perform high complexity clinical laboratory testing. 
 
For additional information, including test methodology,  
please refer to the ADx Test Directory at www.NJLabs.org  
Medical Director: Steve Groshong, M.D., PhD Advanced  
Diagnostic Laboratories, National Jewish Health

commentary 
necessary.

Comment:  
(NOTE) 
 
-------------------ADDITIONAL INFORMATION------------------- 
PLEASE NOTE:  The given thyroglobulin antibody (TgAb)  
reference cutoff of <4.0 IU/mL is for the evaluation of 
autoimmune thyroiditis.  
 
A cutoff of <1.8 IU/mL may be more suitable for 
the detection of potential thyroglobulin antibody (TgAb)  
interference in thyroglobulin immunoassays. 
 
The thyroglobulin antibody testing method is an  
immunoenzymatic assay manufactured by Beckman Coulter Inc.  
and performed on the Unicel DXI 800. 
 
Values obtained from different assay methods or kits 
may be different and cannot be used interchangeably. 
The results cannot be interpreted as absolute evidence for  
the presence or absence of malignant disease.

?Today, I had the pleasure of seeing your patient, Hannah Munguia-Flores at the Allergy and Immunology Clinic at Brigham and 
Women's Hospital.  
 
As you know, Hannah Munguia-Flores is a 29 y.o. female with a history of mild intermittent asthma, chronic fatigue (suspected to 
be from long COVID ) who presents today for evaluation of chronic spontaneous urticaria. 
 
Chronic hives: 
She has had hives all her life 
When she was a child they almost exclusively occurred at night and she managed them with colloidal oatmeal hives with relief. 
Over the years the hives were mild, but since getting COVID the summer of 2022, the hives have worsened in intensity and 
frequency, and more so since last Spring 2024. 
After getting COVID, she tested + for mono. She saw a naturopathic provider and reportedly tested + for Lyme, babesia, bartonella 
and was treated with a course of antibiotics. 
The hives are slightly raised, erythematous, pruritic. Each lesion lasts only a few hours. 
 
She ordered a serum IgE respiratory panel in May 2024 via Quest diagnostics and only tested positive to dust mites (0.18 sIgE). She 
also ordered food IgE testing which was negative. She also ordered Celiac testing which was negative. IgA level was above normal 
range at 414. She had H.pylori testing which was negative in May 2024. 
 
Location: face, trunk, extremities - hives cover a lot of her skin surface.  
Bruising or hyperpigmentation upon resolution: no  
Frequency: currently daily

IMPRESSION AND PLAN: 
Hannah Munguia-Flores is a 29 y.o. female presenting with: 
 
Chronic spontaneous urticaria: 
Her CSU is well controlled on oral antihistamines. We reviewed that CSU is considered a mild form of an autoimmune process, and 
it is treated symptomatically with oral antihistamines. If hives are uncontrolled on maximum doses of oral antihistamines or she is 
unable to tolerate antihistamines, Omalizumab would be an option. There is an established association between urticaria/ 
angioedema and autoimmune thyroiditis, and reassuringly her most recent TSH level is normal. Given her family history of 
autoimminity, however, we will check for TPO and thyroglobulin antibodies which can give us a better idea of her risk to develop 
autoimmune thyroiditis. We will check a chronic urticaria index to see if we can detect auto-antibodies to the Fc?RI, Fc?RII, or IgE 
receptors which are associated with CSU. We reviewed that whether we can identify an autoantibody or not, our management 
would be the same. The natural course of CSU is that it may remit and recur over the years. 
PLAN: 
- Continue Cetirizine 10 mg every other day; may increase to daily if needed 
- Draw labs below:

MGB Small Fiber and Autonomic Neurology Program 
 
Autonomic Reflex Testing Report 
 
Reason for study: Hannah Munguia-Flores is a 29 y.o. female referred by Lauren Major PA for autonomic evaluation for fatigue, 
exercise intolerance, chronic hives, post-COVID fatigue, and autonomic neuropathy. 
 
Location of testing: MGH 
 
The following medications were listed in the patient chart. 
 
Current Outpatient Medications  
Medication Sig Dispense Refill Last Dispense  
ethinyl estradiol/drospirenone (DROSPIRENONE-ETHINYL ESTRADIOL, LORYNA,) 3-0.02 mg per tablet Take 1 tablet by mouth daily. 
Unknown (patient-reported)  
FLUoxetine (PROZAC) 20 MG capsule Take 1 capsule (20 mg total) by mouth daily. 30 capsule 3 Unknown (outside pharmacy)  
METHYLENE BLUE, BULK-SOLID, MISC Take 10 mg by mouth daily. Unknown (patient-reported)  
naltrexone 4.5 mg Cap Take 4.5 mg by mouth daily. Unknown (patient-reported)  
 
No current facility-administered medications for this encounter.  
 
A medication reconciliation was not performed prior to testing.  
 
Results: 
 
Sudomotor function:  
 
QSART (Q-Sweat®) showed normal sweat volume at all sites except the forearm. This pattern can be seen with some kinds of small 
fiber sympathetic sudomotor cholinergic nerve dysfunction, but most commonly is due to interference from medications (e.g. 
antidepressants, anticholinergics) 
 
Cardiovascular autonomic function: 
 
2. Heart rate and Blood Pressure Response to 10 min 70° Head Up Tilt (HUT): 
Baseline supine systolic blood (SBP) pressure was ~ 110 mmHg. SBP after HUT 130 mmHg 
Baseline supine heart rate was ~ 70 beats per minute (bpm), which changed to 85-90 bpm during HUT (normal HR augmentation 
<40 under age 20, <30 in adults) 
 
3. Valsalva Maneuver: 
Blood pressure response to Valsalva Maneuver (sympathetic noradrenergic vasomotor baroreflex function): normal phase 2 and IV ; 
PRT = 1.6 s 
Valsalva ratio: normal at 2.6. (5th percentile: 1.46) 
 
4. Heart Rate Variability to Deep Breathing/HRDB (baroreflex cardiovagal function):  
normal with the best heart rate difference of 22.6 bpm. (5th percentile: 14 bpm) 
 
Conclusions:  
 
These results are essentially normal without evidence of sudomotor dysfunction, cardiovagal or vasomotor baroreflex dysfunction, 
orthostatic hypotension, or excessive orthostatic tachycardia. Patchy low sweat volume is of uncertain significance, but can be seen 


=== PAGE 429 ===
due to interference from medications (e.g. fluoxetine, naltrexone) or more rarely, small fiber sudomotor neuropathy or sweat gland 
dysfunction. 
 
*It is advised that the report needs to be interpreted by the ordering provider or an autonomic expert, taking into account the 
clinical context. 
 
**Please note: published normative data is sparse in pediatric age populations.

Composite Autonomic Scoring Scale (Low 1993, PMID: 8392653)  
Sudomotor  
0 normal  
1 single site abnormal on QSART or  
length-dependent pattern (distal < 1/3 of proximal) or  
persistent sweat activity at foot  
2 single site <50% of lower limit on QSART  
3 Two or more sites < 50% lower limit  
Adrenergic Index  
0 normal  
1 Phase IIe decrease <40 but >20 mmHg mean BP or  
Phase II does not return to baseline or  
Decrease in PP to <50% of baseline  
2 Phase IIe decrease <40 but >20 mmHg mean BP and  
phase II late or phase IV absent  
3 phase IIe decrease of >40 mmHg +no late phase II and phase IV  
4 Criteria for 3 + OH (systolic >30, mean decerased >20)  
Cardiovascular HR index  
0 normal  
1 HRDB or VR mildly decreased (above 50% minimum)  
2 HRDB or VR decreased to less than 50% minimum  
3 Both HRDB and VR < 50% minimum  
Total = 2  
>=5 suggests significant autonomic dysfunction MILD = 1-3 MOD = 4-6 Sev = 7-10  
 
Techniques and procedures used in the MGH Autonomic Lab for Autonomic Reflex Screen Testing  
 
CNAP® (Continuous Noninvasive Arterial Pressure) with double finger sensor technology, is used for recording continuous (beat- 
to-beat) blood pressure. 3-Lead continuous ECG monitoring is used throughout the testing.  
 
Q-Sweat test is performed for evaluation of the integrity of the postganglionic sympathetic sudomotor fibers (primarily 
cholinergic), with the use of 10% acetylcholine solution iontophoresis technique to measure indirect reflex sweat response through 
changes in humidity, recorded from 4 standard locations of ventral forearm, upper lateral shin, lower medial shin and dorsal foot. 
 
70° Head-Up-Tilt is performed after 10 minutes of resting quietly in supine position with stable baseline BP and HR. Then the 
patient is tilted-up to 70°, and is returned to supine after 10 min or until patient experiences problematic severe orthostatic 
intolerance, presyncopal symptoms, or syncope, in which case the test is terminated prematurely. 
 
Valsalva Maneuver is performed in supine position with expiratory pressure to be maintained ideally at 40 mmHg for 15 seconds by 
blowing through a disposable COVID-filtered mouthpiece attached to a digital manometer built in WR medical autonomic testing 
equipment. If this pressure cannot be maintained, lesser pressures are acceptable only if they are sufficient to elicit appropriate 
physiologic responses. The test is done at least twice with 2 min rest period in between, to analyze blood pressure changes and 
pulse valsalva ratio of the best recording. In the case of a table top/flat top/square wave response or other technical challenges, 
this maneuver may be repeated again sitting up. 
 
Heart rate variability with deep breathing is performed in supine position, after a 5 min period of resting, with 5 seconds inspiration 
and 5 seconds expiration cycles for 1 minute (6 breaths per minute) using timed breathing cues. The test is done twice with 2 min 
rest period in between, to analyze the best heart rate changes recorded for a 5 consecutive breathing cycles.

Narrative
A medication reconciliation was not performed prior to testing.  
 
 
Results: 
 
Sudomotor function:  
 
QSART (Q-Sweat®) showed normal sweat volume at all sites except  
the forearm.  This pattern can be seen with some kinds of small  
fiber sympathetic sudomotor cholinergic nerve dysfunction, but  
most commonly is due to interference from medications (e.g.  
antidepressants, anticholinergics) 
 
Cardiovascular autonomic function: 
 
2. Heart rate and Blood Pressure Response to 10 min 70° Head Up  
Tilt (HUT): 
Baseline supine systolic blood (SBP) pressure was ~ 110 mmHg. SBP  
after HUT 130 mmHg 
Baseline supine heart rate was ~ 70 beats per minute (bpm), which  
changed to 85-90 bpm during HUT (normal HR augmentation <40 under  
age 20, <30 in adults) 
 
 
3.  Valsalva Maneuver: 
Blood pressure response to Valsalva Maneuver (sympathetic  
noradrenergic vasomotor baroreflex function):  normal phase 2 and  
IV  ; PRT = 1.6 s 
Valsalva ratio:  normal at 2.6. (5th percentile: 1.46) 
 
4.  Heart Rate Variability to Deep Breathing/HRDB (baroreflex  
cardiovagal function):  
 normal with the best heart rate difference of 22.6 bpm. (5th  
percentile: 14 bpm) 
 
 
Conclusions:  
 
These results are essentially normal without evidence of  
sudomotor dysfunction, cardiovagal or vasomotor baroreflex  
dysfunction, orthostatic hypotension, or excessive orthostatic  
tachycardia.  Patchy low sweat volume is of uncertain  
significance, but can be seen due to interference from  
medications (e.g. fluoxetine, naltrexone) or more rarely, small  
fiber sudomotor neuropathy or sweat gland dysfunction. 
 
*It is advised that the report needs to be interpreted by the  
ordering provider or an autonomic expert, taking into account the  
clinical context. 
 
**Please note: published normative data is sparse in pediatric  
age populations.

Techniques and procedures used in the MGH Autonomic Lab for  
Autonomic Reflex Screen Testing  
 
 
CNAP® (Continuous Noninvasive Arterial Pressure) with double  
finger sensor technology, is used for recording continuous  
(beat-to-beat) blood pressure. 3-Lead continuous ECG monitoring  
is used throughout the testing.  
 
Q-Sweat test is performed for evaluation of the integrity of the  
postganglionic sympathetic sudomotor fibers (primarily  
cholinergic), with the use of 10% acetylcholine solution  
iontophoresis technique to measure indirect reflex sweat response  
through changes in humidity, recorded from 4 standard locations  
of ventral forearm, upper lateral shin, lower medial shin and  
dorsal foot. 
 
70° Head-Up-Tilt is performed after 10 minutes of resting quietly  
in supine position with stable baseline BP and HR. Then the  
patient is tilted-up to 70°, and is returned to supine after 10  
min or until patient experiences problematic severe orthostatic  
intolerance, presyncopal symptoms, or syncope, in which case the  
test is terminated prematurely. 
 
Valsalva Maneuver is performed in supine position with expiratory  
pressure to be maintained ideally at 40 mmHg for 15 seconds by  
blowing through a disposable COVID-filtered mouthpiece attached  
to a digital manometer built in WR medical autonomic testing  
equipment. If this pressure cannot be maintained, lesser  
pressures are acceptable only if they are sufficient to elicit  
appropriate physiologic responses. The test is done at least  
twice with 2 min rest period in between, to analyze blood  
pressure changes and pulse valsalva ratio of the best recording.  
In the case of a table top/flat top/square wave response or other  
technical challenges, this maneuver may be repeated again sitting  
up. 
 
Heart rate variability with deep breathing is performed in supine  
position, after a 5 min period of resting, with 5 seconds  
inspiration and 5 seconds expiration cycles for 1 minute (6  
breaths per minute) using timed breathing cues. The test is done  
twice with 2 min rest period in between, to analyze the best  
heart rate changes recorded for a 5 consecutive breathing cycles.

Assessment & Plan 
Autonomic dysfunction: 
Given the constellation of symptoms including hives, neuropathy, brain fog at times, and post exertional fatigue, all symptoms 
suggestive of autonomic dysfunction.  
We reviewed the pathophysiology in detail with the patient today.  
We provided an educational document that goes over this, in addition to some tips and behavioral changes including proper 
hydration, salt intake to at least 2000-8000 mg per day or 4 teaspoons, compression stocking and modified exercise regimen with 
emphasis on recumbent exercises and pacing strategies.  
Graded exercise program = gentle exercise every day, not so much to make you crash, but a little bit consistently every day then 
extend it once or twice a week by increasing *either* the duration or the intensity a little bit 
- gentle walks  
- recumbent exercise (jumping jacks on your back, recumbent bicycle, etc)  
- swimming or walking in the shallow end of the pool 
- AVOID exercises where you are holding your breath (push-ups, sit-ups, etc) 
- Pacing yourself: Spoon Theory 
- Recommend taking cognitive breaks, for about 1 minute every 30-60 minutes 
- importance of hydration and salt intake 
- compression stockings (15-20 mmHg)  
- resources: packet on Chronic Fatigue Syndrome / Myalgic Encephalopathy; website www.dysautonomiainternational.org  
Recommend considering participation in Long Covid clinical trials, including RECOVER trials available in CRC resources 
We will refer them to Long-Covid PT and cognitive rehab 
We will order AFT 
 
We will refer to Dr. Gupta in NP based on hx of ADHD and recommendations moving forward. 
 
In the future, we can consider referral to pain med for stellate ganglion block, 
Or to Osher Center as well 
 
Continues to follow with NT as well, on 4.5mg LDN for 1 month now. 
 
We discussed diagnostic work up including level 3 CPET, and neuro autonomic function testing including tilt table and skin biopsy. 
We could pursue if continues to have symptoms despite above interventions. 
 
Endorses consistent hives as well, will look into referral for MCAS. Recommend continue antihistamine therapy, consider 
transitioning from Zyrtec to Allegra in the future. 
 
We will order a set of labs to assess for contributing factors of fatigue including CBC and diff, vitamin D, vitamin B12, TSH. 
 
I personally spent a total of 19 minutes on care for this patient on the date of the encounter. This includes face-to-face time during 
the visit as well as non face-to-face time spent on chart review, documentation, and care coordination.

patient will follow up in clinic in 1-2 months. I advised the patient to contact me with any questions or concerns.

recommendations will be stated below.

developing diabetes. The diagnostic HbA1c level for diabetes is 6.5% or greater. HbA1c levels <4.2% may indicate a

Hannah Munguia-Flores is a 29 y.o. female of Rozynek, Veronica, MD who presents for an urgent care visit.

HPI 
 
29 yr old F PMHx long COVID depression presents for follow up.  
 
Patient has long-standing history of fatigue that started 2.5 years ago after she had COVID and Lyme disease. 
 
She had COVID 3x (July 2022, August 2023, Sept 2024)  
 
Being followed outpatient at long COVID syndrome due to consistent fatigue. 
 
Patient voices " something feels wrong with her body on a molecular level" and " is worried about rheumatoid arthritis and SLE". 
 
Patient reports fatigue continues to be debilitating.  
 
Recently discovered there is mold present in her apartment and patient requesting letter of medical necessity to break her lease. 
 
Will get lab work done after this visit per discussion in last visit to rule out thyroid, infectious , endocrine causes of fatigue. 
 
Patient is still on medical leave from school. Not able to exercise or work due to consistent , chronic every day fatigue. 
 
Overall sleeping well and eating well, being tapered off SSRI to from original dose of 60 mg OD. 
 
Patient requests to have full STD screening performed.
